- `scripts/make-app.sh debug` now writes `NSAudioCaptureUsageDescription` into the dev bundle (`SA_OMIT_AUDIO_KEY=1` omits it). The Info.plist `LSMinimumSystemVersion` is still 14.0 (the SwiftPM minimum); the probe reports "unavailable" on a system without `AudioHardwareCreateProcessTap` (14.2).

### Known limitations and open questions of Step 6a (stated, not measured)
- **Everything about the real tap is UNVERIFIED**: whether `AudioHardwareCreateProcessTap` succeeds for an unprompted process, whether the tap-only private aggregate device creates and starts (the fallback `--tap-probe-with-output-device` exists for that case), the tap's format and cadence, whether this process has an audio process object (if not, nothing is excluded and the report says so), the permission prompt text and timing, the behavior when denied, whether the key is needed, whether the original audio stays audible, and what protected (DRM) content does (it may be silent; recorded, never worked around). **[Update 2026-11-08: most of this was answered by real runs: 17 real tap runs on 2026-11-07 ("Step 6a hardware findings") and the six real ring-probe runs of 2026-11-08 ("Step 6b-2 real runs"), in which `AudioHardwareCreateProcessTap` returned status 0 and the tap format was read (lpcm, flags 0x9, 2 channels, 48 kHz) in all six, the tap-only aggregate device started, and this process had an audio process object. Still NOT answered: the permission prompt text and timing (never recorded in any run, 6a or 6b-2), the behavior when denied, whether the key is needed, and protected content.]**
- **Callback-state lifetime** against a late callback is not settled (the header does not say that destroying the IO proc waits for a running callback): the real backend never frees the record buffer; the process exits right after the run. Step 6b needs a protocol.
- **Teardown verification** inside the process (lists before / while running / after, with polling) is the strong evidence; from another process it can only show that nothing visible remains, because the tap and the aggregate device are private.
- The record buffer is sized for at most 1,000 callbacks per second and 600 s; a faster cadence drops records (counted and reported).
- A wrapped or zero frame divisor is undefined behaviour that arm64 turns into 0, so `swift test` cannot see it; only `scripts/ubsan-tapprobe.sh` can. That script is not part of `swift test` and must be run by hand (as must the scan and TSan scripts). It was written because the original guard missed the wrapped 32-bit product.
- The simulated source's callback jitter (thread sleep granularity) says nothing about Core Audio's.

**Not run (needs Sanjai, the permission prompt and real audio).** The whole of docs/MANUAL_TEST_STEP6A.md: the first run with the prompt, the denied path, the optional no-key run, nothing-playing and protected-content runs, the `--list-audio-devices` check after a real run. Everything in Step 6a that touches a real tap is **UNVERIFIED**.

## Step 6a2 findings and verification record (2026-11-06): device, clock and rate facts in the probe
**Scope.** Read-only measurement added to the Step 6a probe: no muting, no output rendering, no mute-safety experiment (those get their own step because they can leave the Mac silent), no permission beyond the tap, and **the real-time callback was not changed** (`git diff` of `Sources/CTapProbe` and `scripts/` is empty), so the scan, TSan and UBSan scripts were re-run as a regression check and not re-broken. The real tap has still never been run.

**What was added.** (1) Property reads, getters only (names verified in the SDK headers: `kAudioObjectPropertyName`, `kAudioDevicePropertyTransportType`, `...NominalSampleRate`, `...ActualSampleRate`, `...Latency`, `...SafetyOffset`, `kAudioStreamPropertyLatency`, `...BufferFrameSize`, `...ClockDomain`, `...ClockDevice`, `...Streams`): the default output device before anything is created, and the tap's aggregate device while it runs (input-scope latencies). The status of every read is kept and an unreadable value stays empty. The device UID is never recorded (it can contain a hardware address); the NAME is recorded because it was asked for, and it can contain a person's name. (2) The run waits in 10 s slices and reads `ActualSampleRate` of both devices at the start and after every slice. (3) The callback records are grouped in 10 s windows: implied frames per second, parts per million against the declared rate, and the cumulative frame offset (frames delivered minus declared rate x host time). (4) A side-by-side rate comparison (tap declared rate against the output nominal rate, tap implied rate against the output actual-rate readings). (5) Report schema 2.

**What a tap-only aggregate does and does not tell us about clocks (printed in every report, section 4).** The probe's aggregate has no sub-device and no clock device, so the headers do not say which clock its callbacks follow, and the probe does not decide that. The cadence is measured against the HOST clock: it shows whether the callbacks are regular and whether their frame rate matches the declared rate against the host clock; it does NOT show whether the tap and the output device share a clock. `ClockDomain`: the same non-zero value means "able to be synchronized in hardware", 0 means unspecified and separate (header); equal non-zero would not prove synchronization. `ActualSampleRate` is "as measured by its time stamps" (header); that this is against the host clock is a reading of those words, UNVERIFIED. **No drift claim without a measured series:** the 10 s windows and the cumulative offset of a long run are that series; one reading or a short run supports none, and a SIMULATED run supports none at all (the 25 s simulated launch showed -284 ppm in its first window and a -136 frame offset purely from the test thread's sleep jitter). The offset has noise of about one callback's scheduling jitter, because the callbacks' `inNow` timestamps include it (it does not accumulate).

**One real read-only observation (this Mac, macOS 27.2, `--list-audio-devices`; property values, NOT a latency measurement and not about the tap).** Default output device: built-in speakers (transport `bltn`, kAudioDeviceTransportTypeBuiltIn), nominal rate 48,000.0 Hz, actual rate 48,000.000 Hz, buffer 512 frames, 0 input / 2 output channels, device latency 60 frames, safety offset 48 frames, stream latency 556 frames (output scope), clock domain 1835100526 (non-zero), no clock device named, no read with a non-zero status. Whether the tap's aggregate device reports a clock domain, and whether it equals that one, is exactly what the real run will show.

**Long run and its cost (docs/MANUAL_TEST_STEP6A.md, section 4b).** `caffeinate -i open -n -W ... --args --tap-probe 600`: ten-plus minutes of continuous non-protected audio, the Mac awake, no output-device changes unless deliberate, no window or progress display; a sleep or a device change shows as a gap in the windows. The record buffer is sized for about 600,000 records of 48 bytes (32 bytes before Step 6a3), about 29 MB, from the sizes in the code, not measured; the JSON of a 600 s run is estimated at 150 to 200 KB (not measured).

**Verification record.**
- `swift build` (debug and release, no warnings); `swift test` **528 tests** (430 Core + 58 Engine + 40 Tracking; two opt-in tests skipped by default); the same under `swift test --sanitize=thread` with no race reports.
- New tests: window bucketing and arithmetic (exact steady rate, a +100 frames/s offset gives +2083.333 ppm and an offset that grows by one frame per callback, a slow tap gives negative ppm and offset, a callback exactly on a boundary starts the next window, gaps stay visible as jumps in `startSecond`, no declared rate or no clock gives empty fields, the window length is a parameter); transport names from the header; failed reads; the runner (slices 10 / 10 / 5 for 25 s, rate samples at the start and after each slice with elapsed times, a stop request ends the run at a slice boundary, output facts read before anything is created and still read after a failure or a refusal, aggregate facts read while running and never after a failed start, the rate comparison with exact means and ppm, unequal nominal rates noted, unreadable facts named and not fatal); the report (section 4 present, every clock statement printed, schema 2 round trip); and a 25 s burst on the simulated source (three windows at the synthetic rate, facts labelled SIMULATED, roles and scopes right). No test uses a clock or a fixed sleep.
- **Mutation check: 33 mutants on the new logic** (window index, span, implied rate, ppm sign, cumulative offset, completeness, window length, frame count, start second; slicing, per-slice and initial samples, stop request, aggregate and output facts, equality flag, mean, ppm, notes, elapsed times; transport names, failed reads, the clock statement and its printing, schema version, the window table; two simulated-source mutants). First run: 32 killed, **1 survivor (E01: the simulated aggregate facts claimed the wrong role): my test checked the device names but never the `role` label, which is what the markdown prints.** Fixed with an assertion on roles, scope and the markdown line. **Final run on the final tests: 33/33 killed by `swift test`, all files byte-identical afterwards.** After a later formatting-only change (sub-lines of the facts), the three report-section mutants were re-run: 3/3 killed.
- Scripts re-run on the unchanged callback: `scripts/scan-rt-callback.sh` (82 instructions, 0 calls), `scripts/tsan-tapprobe.sh` (200,000 records, clean), `scripts/ubsan-tapprobe.sh` (exit 0). Their broken variants were not re-run because the callback did not change.

The 24 are the harness's mutants; K12 (dropped from the Step 2f harness) is a survivor, so the figure is **24 of 25**, not 23 of 24. ### Known limitations of Step 6a2 (stated, not measured)
- Everything about the real tap, its aggregate device's clock domain and rates is UNVERIFIED: only the simulated source and the one read-only look at the output device have been seen. The property reader for the aggregate device has never read a real aggregate device.
- The cadence windows use the callbacks' `inNow` host time, which includes scheduling jitter; `inInputTime` ("the time at which the first frame in the data was acquired") might be a better timestamp for drift, but using it means changing the real-time callback, which this step avoided. **Done in Step 6a3 (below), before any real run.**
- `ActualSampleRate` is read from the control thread at 10 s intervals; the header does not say over what interval the HAL measures it.
- The device name can be personal; the report records it as asked.
- The 10 s windows do not detect a drift smaller than the window-to-window noise; a trend needs the long run and judgement, and none is claimed.

**Not run (needs Sanjai, the permission prompt and real audio).** The whole of docs/MANUAL_TEST_STEP6A.md including the new long run. Everything in Step 6a2 that touches a real tap or a real aggregate device is **UNVERIFIED**.

## Step 6a3 findings and verification record (2026-11-07): input timestamps and trustworthy drift data

**Scope.** Make the 600 s run's drift data readable before it is spent. One change to the real-time callback (record `inInputTime` next to `inNow`), a side-by-side report, the callback's checks re-run with fresh broken variants, and a revision of the two-block ring design. No muting, no output, no new permission; the real tap has still never been run.

**What the headers say (read in this step).** `inNow` "indicates the IO cycle started ... includes any scheduling latency that may have been incurred waking the thread"; `inInputTime` "indicates the time at which the first frame in the data was acquired from the hardware. If the device has no input streams, the time stamp will be zeroed out" (AudioHardware.h). Validity is in `mFlags`: `kAudioTimeStampSampleTimeValid` = bit 0, `kAudioTimeStampHostTimeValid` = bit 1, `kAudioTimeStampRateScalarValid` = bit 2 (CoreAudioBaseTypes.h). The headers do not say which of them a tap's aggregate device sets.

**The callback change (and only this).** `SATapProbeRecord` grew from 32 to 48 bytes: `inputHostTime` (u64) and `inputSampleTime` (double), plus three bits in the existing `flags`: no `inInputTime` pointer (32), host time not valid (64), sample time not valid (128). Validity comes from `mFlags` only, never from the value (a sample time of 0 is legitimate); an invalid field is stored as 0 so garbage never reaches the report; a NULL `inInputTime` sets all three bits. The release object is 101 instructions (82 before), 0 calls, no branch out. **Not recorded, named as candidates:** the input `mRateScalar` ("the ratio of actual host ticks per sample frame to the nominal host ticks per sample frame", marked valid by its own bit) and the validity of `inNow` itself (the callback has always read `inNow->mHostTime` without looking at `inNow->mFlags`; this step did not change that, by instruction). The record buffer for a 600 s run is about 600,000 x 48 bytes = 29 MB (from the sizes in the code, not measured).

**The report (schema 3).** The window table has `inNow` and `input` columns for the SAME callbacks (windows are bucketed by `inNow` for both, so a difference is the stamp's, not the membership's): implied frames/s, ppm against the declared rate, cumulative frame offset, plus the number of usable input callbacks per window. For each estimator over the whole run: a least-squares fit of the offset (frames delivered minus declared rate x stamp time, from the estimator's own first callback) against stamp time: the slope as ppm of the declared rate with one standard error, the SD and peak-to-peak of the offset about the line in frames, and **the resolution floor**: `peak-to-peak / (declared rate x time) x 1e6` ppm, for the whole run and for one 10 s window. Also: how many callbacks had a valid input host time / sample time / no pointer, `inNow` minus input host time (ms, min / median / max), and sample-time continuity (did the HAL's counter advance by exactly the frames delivered). Sentences in words are generated from these numbers and say "NOT larger than the jitter" when the fitted offset change over the run is not larger than the scatter.

**What each estimator can and cannot resolve (printed in every report).** Both compare the tap's frames with a HOST-clock timestamp per callback: the tap against the host clock, nothing about the output device's clock. `inNow` carries the scheduling variation; the input host time may be steadier but what it refers to for a tap-only aggregate is not documented, and whether the HAL smooths it with its own clock model is UNVERIFIED, so a very straight input line is NOT proof of a steady clock. **Resolution is arithmetic on the formula, not a measurement of this Mac:** at 48,000 Hz over 600 s one frame of peak-to-peak scatter is 0.0347 ppm (so 1 ms = 48 frames = 1.67 ppm), and within one 10 s window one frame is 2.08 ppm (so 1 ms = 100 ppm). What the scatter really is on this Mac is unmeasured: the run will say. A drift smaller than the printed floor cannot be told from jitter; a short run has a large floor; the standard error assumes independent jitter (UNVERIFIED), the floor does not. Not resolved by either: the output device's drift, tap-versus-output drift, a change of the HAL's own rate during the run (folded into the slope), non-linear drift (the window table shows it). The older whole-run "tap implied" rate on the Rates line uses only the first and last callback, so it is labelled an ENDPOINT estimate in the report (the simulated 25 s run printed -99.7 ppm there and +2.80 ppm from the fit, which is exactly the kind of disagreement the label is for).

**Simulated example (FABRICATED, decides nothing).** The simulated source stamps `inInputTime` on the ideal timeline minus a fixed latency, so its input estimator shows 0.0 frames of scatter and its `inNow` estimator shows the pacing thread's sleep jitter (220 frames peak-to-peak over 25 s, floor 183 ppm in that run). This only shows that the report computes and prints both; nothing about a real tap follows from it.

**Verification record.**
- `swift build` (debug and release, no warnings). `swift test` **578 tests** (470 Core + 68 Engine + 40 Tracking; two opt-in tests skipped by default; +50 on Step 6a2's 528), the same under `swift test --sanitize=thread` with no race reports. No new test uses a clock or a sleep (checked by grep; the one hit for "Clock" is the `wordClockTimeValid` timestamp flag name).
- New tests: the callback's recording of both fields (values beyond 32 bits and float precision, independence from `inNow`, every validity combination, other `mFlags` bits ignored, a sample time of 0 valid, garbage not kept, a NULL `inInputTime`, a NULL `inNow`, per-callback order); Core's copy of each flag bit pinned to the C macro; the fit on hand-computed series (offsets 0,1,0,1,0 give SD sqrt(0.4), s.e. 200 ppm, floor 250 / 100 ppm), a straight line at +200 / -100 ppm, the floor scaling with time, guards, and invariance under a constant shift of frames or time; the two estimators on the same callbacks (identical stamps give identical results, a constant latency cancels, jitter only in `inNow`, an input clock 100.01 ppm fast, invalid callbacks left out but their frames still counted, a window with no usable input, a last-in-window callback without input, varying block sizes with exact stamps, the window length reaching the floor); sample-time continuity (gap, overlap, half-frame tolerance, the earlier callback's frames, invalid and non-finite sample times); validity from flags field by field; the latency figure; the report (schema 3 round trip, a schema-2 JSON still decodes, both tables' cell contents, the limits, the statements both ways, the dashes when there is no input time, the Rates line's endpoint note); and the simulated source end to end through the real callback.
- **A branch with no test since Step 6a, found by this work:** `SA_TAP_FLAG_NO_TIMESTAMP` (a NULL `inNow`) was never tested because Swift's import of the callback forbids NULL. The tests now call the same C function through an ABI-identical type with optional pointers; the mutant that drops the flag is killed.
- **Callback checks re-run on the changed callback, with broken variants regenerated from the changed source (each patch applied exactly once):** scan: the real object PASSES (101 instructions, 0 calls); the control passes and **4 of 4** broken variants FAIL (malloc, malloc placed only in the new NULL-input branch, a 512-byte struct copy, a mutex). TSan (`scripts/tsan-tapprobe.sh`, whose harness now cycles all four validity combinations per call and checks both new fields): the real source is clean (200,000 records, 0 failures, no race report); **10 of 10** broken variants FAIL: four with ThreadSanitizer data-race reports (publish before write, relaxed publish, relaxed consumer, input fields written after the publish) and six by wrong-record detection naming the field (the read skips the input host time, the read skips the input sample time, the host time copied from `inNow`, validity taken from the value, garbage kept when invalid, the host validity tested with the wrong bit). UBSan / hostile input (`scripts/ubsan-tapprobe.sh`, which now also feeds a NULL `inInputTime`, a NULL `inNow` and both): the real source passes; **5 of 5** broken variants FAIL (zero sample size accepted and the 32-bit product, both with UBSan reports; a NULL `inInputTime` dereference and a NULL `inNow` dereference, which die at that input; the NULL flag bit dropped, wrong result).
- **Mutation check of the new logic: 101 mutants** (callback validity, NULL handling, storage and read-out; header constants and the type of the stored sample time; the record's usability rules; flags; the aggregator's input origin, membership, columns, points and offsets; every formula and guard of the fit; continuity; the latency figure; the report's tables and sentences; the simulated source and the backend reader; the Rates sentence). **First run of 100: 91 killed by `swift test`, 8 survived, 1 did not compile** (C23: the stored sample time as a `float` is a type error on the Swift side: caught by the compiler, not counted as killed by a test). The survivors: **C16** (the input fields written AFTER the record is published): single-threaded tests cannot see a store order; **killed by `scripts/tsan-tapprobe.sh`** (ThreadSanitizer data race, exit 134), which is its designed killer. **Six real test gaps**, each a finding about my tests: R01 and R06 (the Core tests built invalid records with a zero value, so the flag was never what decided; a record with the flag set and a non-zero value, or the wrong field's flag, was untested), S07 (the tests always set all three flag bits together, so `noInputTime` could count the wrong bit), E18 (every series started at time 0, so span = last time passed), T10 and T11 (the fixture gave equal cells for both estimators, so a column printing the other estimator's value was invisible). All six were closed with tests and re-run: killed. Three of them (R01, R06, S07) still showed as survivors on the first re-run only because my harness's test filter did not list the new suite: a survivor can be a harness gap, and I checked the filter before touching the tests again. **A13** (the frame origin dropped from the fit's points) survives and is **not counted as killed**: every point's offset shifts by the same constant and the fit is invariant to that. Evidence in code: a differential run on a fixture with a non-zero origin (the first 7 callbacks without a valid input, varying block sizes, jittered stamps): all window fields identical, whole-run fit fields identical to about 13 significant digits and differing only in the 14th (for example drift 0.07430972197084462 against 0.07430972197084536 ppm), i.e. floating-point rounding; the committed test `movingTheOriginOfTheFramesOrOfTheTimeDoesNotChangeTheFit` asserts the invariance of the estimator. **Final: of 100 mutants with a verdict, 98 killed by `swift test`, 1 killed by a script only (C16), 1 equivalent up to rounding (A13).** The Rates-sentence mutant was added after the first run and killed. All mutated files were byte-identical to before each run (sha-compared).
- Bundle rebuilt (`scripts/make-app.sh debug`); a 25 s SIMULATED launch through it wrote the schema-3 report with the new tables to a scratch folder, stderr empty; nothing in `docs/test-results/` besides the README note.

**Design revision (docs/ARCHITECTURE.md, "Reading the ring from two blocks"; PLANNED / UNVERIFIED, no code).** One producer index; one read cursor per block, written only by its block; each block advances only its own cursor by exactly the frames it was asked for, every call, whether or not data was available, so correctness does not depend on the order (or the concurrency) in which the engine runs the two blocks. Availability is per frame index: not yet written (underrun) or overwritten (overrun) reads as zeros. Divergence is detected by phase, `cursor - render timestamp`, which must be equal and constant for both blocks; the timestamp may be invalid with downstream rate conversion (header), in which case a weaker rule (`d` must be 0 or one frame count) only counts. Policy: underrun = zeros and advance; overrun = jump the block's own cursor forward to the standing fill; diverged = the behind block jumps forward to the larger phase; anything the render thread cannot resolve = re-prime by the control thread with the engine stopped. The withdrawn draft ("one shared cursor advanced by whichever block runs second") was order-dependent by construction. The test plan (a C harness with both block orders, concurrency, a skipped block, different counts, a lapped reader and broken variants including the withdrawn design) is written down, not built.

### Known limitations of Step 6a3 (stated, not measured)
- Everything about real stamps is UNVERIFIED: whether `inInputTime` is valid for a tap aggregate, whether it is steadier than `inNow` or the HAL's smoothed model, and the real scatter.
- `mRateScalar` and `inNow`'s own validity flags are not recorded. The callback reads `inNow->mHostTime` regardless of its flags (unchanged since Step 6a).
- The peak-to-peak (and so the floor) is sensitive to a single outlier callback (one scheduler stall in 600 s raises it for the whole run); the SD is printed next to it. The standard error assumes independent jitter.
- The fit is a straight line: a rate that changes during the run, or a step, is folded into the slope and only visible in the window table.
- Window membership is by `inNow`; near a boundary a callback's input stamp may lie in the neighbouring 10 s, so the input window figures describe the same callbacks, not strictly the same input-time interval.
- Both estimators are against the HOST clock only.
- An unmeasured sentence from Step 6a2 ("the offset has noise of about one callback's scheduling jitter") was replaced by a statement that the noise is measured in the estimator table.
- The two-block ring design is a design; its assumptions about the engine (same frame count and render time per cycle, timestamp validity in our graph) are exactly what a first spike with counters must measure.

**Not run (needs Sanjai, the permission prompt and real audio).** The whole of docs/MANUAL_TEST_STEP6A.md, in particular the 600 s run, which must be on the commit whose message starts "Step 6a3:". Everything in Step 6a3 that touches a real tap is **UNVERIFIED**.

## Step 6a hardware findings (2026-11-07): the first real tap runs
**Scope.** What Sanjai's real runs of 2026-11-07 showed. Build b5b6766 ("Step 6a3:"), MacBook Air M4 (Mac16,12), macOS 27.2. Sources are the 17 real (non-SIMULATED) reports in `docs/test-results/`, all `schemaVersion` 3, `source` `coreAudioTap`; **two are committed** (`066e3d5`): `tapprobe-20261107-110518.{md,json}` (the first audible 10 s run, built-in speakers, YouTube audio) and `tapprobe-20261107-200915.{md,json}` (the 600 s run, Date 2026-11-07T19:59:15+05:30, Bluetooth headphones). The other 15 stay untracked on this Mac. Every number below was recomputed from those files (JSON), not copied from a message. Nothing in Sources/, Tests/ or scripts/ changed in this step. The probe measures only: unmuted, nothing rendered, no audio stored. **The Bluetooth device name was withheld by hand (replaced by "Bluetooth headphones (model withheld)" in the committed 600 s report and in this file); the reports are otherwise exactly as the app produced them.**

**a. Format (all 17 real runs, speakers and Bluetooth).** `lpcm`, 32 bits per channel, 2 channels, 48,000 Hz, `formatFlags` 0x9 = `kAudioFormatFlagIsFloat | kAudioFormatFlagIsPacked` with no `kAudioFormatFlagIsNonInterleaved` (0x20) bit (flag values read in `CoreAudioBaseTypes.h`): **Float32, interleaved**, 8 bytes per frame, 1 frame per packet. Frames per callback: min = median = max = 512 in every run. The tap stayed at 48 kHz while the Bluetooth output ran at 44.1 kHz. The simulated source's default (0x29, non-interleaved) therefore differs from the real tap. Source: all 17 JSON files. **IMPLEMENTED (verified) for this Mac.**

**b. Setup and teardown (all 17 real runs).** All 18 Core Audio calls returned status 0 (including `AudioHardwareCreateProcessTap`, `AudioHardwareCreateAggregateDevice`, `AudioDeviceCreateIOProcID`, `AudioDeviceStart`, and the destroy calls) and `teardownClean` is true in every run. **The tap-only aggregate device (no sub-device, no clock device) starts.** `kAudioHardwarePropertyTranslatePIDToProcessObject` (call 3 of 18, before the tap, the aggregate device and the IO proc existed) returned an audio process object in every run, so a probe-only process, which does no audio IO of its own, had one before any audio IO of ours; it was excluded from the tap (one excluded object per run). Teardown evidence is the in-process lists and per-call statuses (private objects cannot be seen from another process); the `--list-audio-devices` check from a fresh process is not recorded. **IMPLEMENTED (verified) for this Mac.**

**c. Audible capture.**
- *Speakers, 10 s* (`tapprobe-20261107-110518`, YouTube audio): 938 callbacks, peak 0.6048, RMS 0.0703, silent seconds 0 of 10. Per-second peaks rose from 0.14 to 0.19 for nine seconds and were 0.605 in the last; per-second RMS -25.4 to -27.2 dBFS, then -15.5 dBFS. (Sanjai: YouTube; what exactly played is not recorded.)
- *Bluetooth, 600 s* (`tapprobe-20261107-200915`): peak 0.7849, RMS 0.1177. **Silent seconds 34 of 601 per-second rows: seconds 0 to 33, contiguous**; the first audible second is 34 (94 callbacks, peak 0.5100, RMS 0.1553). Sanjai: the music started later than the probe, which fits the contiguous block. No silent second after 33; the lowest later per-second RMS is -38.2 dBFS (second 466), a quiet passage and not silence. Row 600 is a partial second (6 callbacks), hence 601 rows.
- *Other uncommitted runs* (not interpreted here): nine speaker runs (seven of 10 s, two of 5 s) from 10:45 to 11:03 are silent (causes not recorded); **the 11:03 run has no known cause** (Sanjai does not know what was playing); on the Bluetooth device the 19:24:07 run is silent and 19:25:58 has 4 silent seconds of 10 (causes not recorded); the 60 s run of 20:28:48 has 59 silent seconds of 60 (one audible second, second 10, peak 0.198; cause not recorded).

**d. The 600 s run (Bluetooth, `tapprobe-20261107-200915`).** Requested 600 s, elapsed 600.076 s, stopped by timeout, outcome ok, teardown clean. **56,257 callbacks, 0 dropped, no flags set**, 28,803,584 frames, implied 48,000.0012 frames/s; callback interval min / median / max 10.513 / 10.666 / 10.818 ms. **Sample-time continuity: 56,256 consecutive pairs, 0 mismatches.** Windows: 61 rows, 60 complete (937 or 938 callbacks each) and one partial of 6 callbacks. **Cumulative `inNow` offset over the 60 complete windows: +0.20 to +1.54 frames** (first window +1.14, last +0.81); the per-window `inNow` rate against declared ranged -2.2 to +2.5 ppm. Whole-run `inNow` fit: fitted slope +0.0012 ppm (printed 1 s.e. 0.0002, see f), offset peak-to-peak 7.65 frames (SD 0.370), **resolution floor 0.27 ppm over the whole run and 15.9 ppm within one 10 s window**, fitted offset change +0.03 frames. **This compares the tap's callbacks with the HOST clock only; it says nothing about the output device's clock or about a tap-versus-output difference.** That the tap's cadence follows the host clock this closely is consistent with a tap-only aggregate (no clock device, clock domain 0) being paced by host time rather than by audio hardware; **that is a hypothesis, UNVERIFIED**, and if true it makes this result less informative about the real drift problem. The report's rate-comparison line is not meaningful here: tap 48 kHz against output 44.1 kHz are different rates, and the mean output rate it printed (43,639 Hz) and "+99,925 ppm" are distorted by the single 16,000 Hz reading (h).

**e. Input timestamps (all 17 real runs).** `inInputTime` host time and sample time were marked valid on every callback (usable = callbacks in every run), sample-time mismatches 0, and the input estimator is flat: offset peak-to-peak between 5.8e-11 and 7.45e-9 frames in all 17 runs (1.2e-10 in the 10 s runs, 7.45e-9 in the 600 s run; 0.0 at the report's precision; one host tick is 0.002 frames at 48 kHz). **So the input timestamps carry no information about drift in this configuration.** Hypothesis, **UNVERIFIED**: the HAL derives them from the nominal rate (the implied rate is exactly 48,000 to 1e-12 ppm); smoothing by the HAL is not excluded. `mRateScalar` is not recorded, so the HAL's own rate ratio was not seen.

**f. Standard-error caveat.** The five 10 s speaker runs from 10:53 to 11:05 give `inNow` slopes **+0.258, -0.120, -0.642, +0.366, -0.262 ppm** (the values quoted to Sanjai's brief, +0.26 / -0.12 / -0.64 / +0.37 / -0.26, match); the printed 1 s.e. is 0.086 to 0.092. **Four of the five were silent (10:53:38, 10:55:51, 10:57:23, 11:03:30); the fifth, 11:05:08, is the audible committed run.** The range is **1.008 ppm** (-0.642 to +0.366) and the sample SD 0.41 ppm (the "about 0.4" in the brief is the SD, not the range); across all thirteen 10 s runs the range is 1.015 ppm and the SD 0.345 ppm, against printed 1 s.e. of 0.080 to 0.100. **The standard error assumes independent jitter (UNVERIFIED) and understates the scatter between runs by a factor of about four; use the resolution floor, not the s.e.**

**g. Devices.**
- *Built-in speakers* (`tapprobe-20261107-110518`): "MacBook Air Speakers", transport `bltn`, nominal 48,000 Hz, actual 48,000.000 Hz, buffer 512 frames, device-reported latency property 60 frames, safety offset 48 frames, stream latency 556 frames, clock domain 1835100526, no clock device. These are property values, not measurements.
- *Bluetooth* (the Bluetooth headphones, model withheld; the 600 s run and the other Bluetooth runs): transport `blue`, nominal 44,100 Hz, buffer 512, **clock domain 0**, no clock device, safety offset 0, stream latency 0, **device-reported latency property 4,920 frames (a property value, not a measurement)**. The tap aggregate device: nominal and `ActualSampleRate` 48,000, **clock domain 0**, no clock device, latency properties 0, `ActualSampleRate` 48,000 at every reading of every run (61 readings in the 600 s run); the speakers' output `ActualSampleRate` is 48,000 at every reading of every speaker run. Per the header a clock domain of 0 is "unspecified" and is to be treated as separate from every other device; it settles nothing about synchronization. The tap (48 kHz) differs from the Bluetooth output (44.1 kHz), so **rate conversion downstream is the normal case on that device** (the report says so in its notes).

**h. Event in the 600 s run: output `ActualSampleRate` reads 16,000 Hz once.** The readings are every 10 s: 44,100 at every sample except **16,000 at elapsed 220.0 s**. Sanjai switched an input/output setting for a few seconds during that run and did not note the time, so the link is likely and not recorded. Probable cause (inference, **UNVERIFIED**): a Bluetooth profile switch; no header read here states that a profile uses 16 kHz. **What the tap's data show around seconds 205 to 240 (read-only, from the JSON): nothing.** Every second has 93 or 94 callbacks (the same pattern as the rest of the run: 149 seconds with 93 and 450 with 94 over seconds 1 to 599, none outside), no second is silent or unusually quiet (per-second RMS between -15.6 and -24.9 dBFS in 205 to 240), no sample-time mismatch in the run, no flag, the windows at 210, 220 and 230 s are ordinary (`inNow` -0.09, -0.81 and +1.60 ppm; cumulative offset +1.06, +0.86, +1.54 frames), and the aggregate reads 48,000 throughout. Limits: the rate is sampled every 10 s, so a change shorter than that, or a second one, could be missed; the output rate changed while the tap, which is a tap on the system mix at 48 kHz, kept delivering uninterrupted.

**i. Permission.**
- *System log (read-only, `log show` of `com.apple.TCC`, no file changed):* for the first real run (pid 91078, 10:45:22) tccd logged `AUTHREQ_PROMPTING ... service=kTCCServiceAudioCapture` at 10:45:22.836 and `AUTHREQ_RESULT ... authValue=2, authReason=2` at 10:45:24.916, followed by a TCC `Create` event for the same service and our bundle ID; no prompt for later runs. **What `authValue=2` / `authReason=2` mean (allowed, user consent) is TCC convention, UNVERIFIED here; no SDK header read in this project defines it.** The log also shows a separate `TCCAccessLaunchDisclosurePromptIfNeeded` request ("#ManagedTCCDefaults showing the prompt") a moment earlier whose meaning I do not know.
- *Sanjai's screenshot (2026-11-07 20:28):* a purple dot in the menu bar and a Privacy panel listing "SpatialAudio, System Audio Recording".
- **Not recorded:** the prompt text, which button he pressed (he did not remember). **Whether our `NSAudioCaptureUsageDescription` text appeared: UNKNOWN.** The timeline of the first run (createdAt 10:45:22, report file 10:45:34) fits capture waiting about 2 s on the prompt, but no per-call timing exists.

Not yet tested (all remain UNVERIFIED).** The denied path; the run without the Info.plist key; sleep / wake; other device changes (default-output change, Bluetooth connect / disconnect, a deliberate sample-rate change); channel order (the reports hold overall levels only, not left against right); whether the tap is taken before or after the system volume; a tap-versus-OUTPUT-device clock comparison; protected (DRM) content; the `--list-audio-devices` check after a run. The 11:03 silent run has no known cause.

The Step 6a2 and 6a3 rows keep their tags (a note was added to each); the six-line list in ARCHITECTURE.md ("What the real 6a reports must show") is updated: items 1 to 4 answered, items 5 and 6 partly.

## Step 6b-1 findings and verification record (2026-11-07): system-audio ring, render block, scan gate
**Scope.** The real-time core of "system audio to renderer": no camera, no real tap, no app UI, no `EnvironmentRenderer` change, **no audio played**. Wiring is Step 6b-2. The code is **IMPLEMENTED-UNVERIFIED**: built and tested here, never run on hardware, never connected to a real tap or a real-time engine. **No latency, CPU or drift figure exists for it.** The design and what was built: docs/ARCHITECTURE.md ("Step 6b-1: what was built", "Scan rule for the Step 6b block"). Local commits only; nothing pushed.

**Sanjai's decision, and the gate it created.** "Yes, 6b only. I accept the narrow exception for the Step 6b block, provided all five verification cases behave as expected. If any case fails, do not adopt the rule. Keep the exception limited to Step 6b and review it again before Step 8. Nothing should change until the verification is complete." Phase 1 (the block, the scan, the five cases) ran first; the rule was adopted only after all five behaved as required, and nothing in Phase 2 or the docs was done before that. **The exception is for Step 6b only and must be reviewed again before Step 8.**

**The four deliberate departures from the design doc (decided by Sanjai):** (i) the spike COUNTS only (no jump, no re-prime; a block that cannot be fully served writes zeros and counts; signed 64-bit differences); (ii) the producer splits the interleaved tap buffer into two per-channel planes and publishes ONE `w` (release store after the data); (iii) one reader struct per channel, each served by its own mono block; (iv) a control-thread snapshot reads `w` and each published cursor with atomic loads, copies field by field, computes `fill = w - r` once per channel; the render side does only relaxed stores. **Consequence of (i) that matters for any first hardware run:** the cursor advances by exactly `frameCount` on every call, so after an underrun the reader is AHEAD of the producer (negative fill) and, at equal producer and reader rate, stays starved until the producer gets ahead of it (shown in the harness, not measured on an engine). The design's own underrun row (zeros, the cursor still advances by `F`, late frames dropped) implies the same unless the control thread re-primes, and that re-prime is not implemented either (see the limitations).

### Phase 1: the five verification cases (all as required)
Every variant was built from the real sources by a patch that must match exactly once, with the flags SwiftPM uses for this target (`-Os -g -fobjc-arc -std=c11`, read from its build); the unmodified control built that way has a disassembly identical to SwiftPM's objects (517 and 65 lines). For each failing case the line containing the intended call was printed from the SAME object the scan reads, and the scan failed with a message naming that cause. Commands are `scripts/scan-rt-block-cases.sh <case>`; the exit code is the scan's.

| # | Case | Call line in the scanned object (`llvm-objdump -d -r`) | Scan exit | The scan said |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | control: the shipped release objects | block invoke: 15 instructions, one `ARM64_RELOC_BRANCH26 _SARingRender`; callee 144 instructions, 0 calls | 0 | `PASS` (block) and `PASS` (producer: `_SARingIOProc` 88 and `_SARingWrite` 41 instructions, 0 calls) |
| 2 | block calls a DIFFERENT, clean function (`SARingRenderClean`, a copy of the callee; itself shown to pass the zero-call rule) | `bl` + `ARM64_RELOC_BRANCH26 _SARingRenderClean` in `___SACreateSourceNode_block_invoke` | 1 | `[NOT-ALLOW-LISTED]` (and `[CALL-COUNT]`: the allowed callee is never called) |
| 3 | a direct `malloc` in the block (stored through a volatile global) | `bl` + `ARM64_RELOC_BRANCH26 _malloc` in the block invoke | 1 | `[NOT-ALLOW-LISTED]`, `[NOT-DEFINED-IN-OUR-C-OBJECT]` |
| 4 | the allowed callee called twice | two `bl` + `ARM64_RELOC_BRANCH26 _SARingRender` in the block invoke | 1 | `[CALL-COUNT]` (2 call sites) |
| 5 | the allowed callee itself calls `malloc` | `bl` + `ARM64_RELOC_BRANCH26 _malloc` in `_SARingRender` | 1 | `[CALLEE-CALLS]` and `[EXISTING-ZERO-CALL-RULE]` (also: with the real `-Werror=function-effects` the compiler itself rejects it) |

Also run, same method, all failing as required: the render function without its `no_builtin` attribute (`bl _bzero`), and four producer variants (`malloc`; the write helper not inlined, also exercising the tail-call path; a 512-byte struct copy that compiles to `memcpy`; a mutex lock and unlock). After the later source edits (helper refactor, test hooks) all eleven cases were re-run on the final source with the same verdicts, and the shipped object's disassembly was compared against the Phase 1 object after a forced rebuild: identical.

**Follow-up cases 6 and 7 (added after the first report; same method, `scripts/scan-rt-block-cases.sh 6` and `7`; both FAIL as required, exit 1, the scanner unchanged).** Case 6: the block ENDS with `return SARingRenderClean(...)`, which compiles to a block of 2 instructions ending in a plain `b` + `ARM64_RELOC_BRANCH26 _SARingRenderClean` (no `bl`, no code after it); the scan says `[NOT-ALLOW-LISTED]` (and `[CALL-COUNT]`: the allowed callee is never called). Its message reads "calls _SARingRenderClean at 0xb0" and does not say "tail call": the `b` is visible in the disassembly line, and the scanner treats a branch with a call relocation as a call by design. Case 7: the block calls through a volatile function pointer whose value is the allow-listed callee itself; the object has `blr x8` (no relocation); the scan says `[INDIRECT-CALL]` (and `[CALL-COUNT]`), i.e. calling the allowed symbol indirectly does not count as the one allowed call.

### Findings made on the way
- **The scan found a real hidden call in the first release object.** Apple clang turned the zero-fill loop in `SARingRender` into a call to `bzero` (not `memset`). `no_builtin("memset")` did NOT stop it; the bare `__attribute__((no_builtin))` does (tried three spellings against SwiftPM's flags; the unchanged probe reproduced SwiftPM's object, so the probe was faithful). The scan, not the attribute, is the safety net: removing the attribute is now case `z`.
- **SwiftPM compiles C targets with `-Os`, not `-O2`.** The 6a script's `--source` mode uses `-O2`; the variants here use SwiftPM's flags. The design doc's "17 instructions" for the block was a scratch `-O2` spike; the shipped block is 15 instructions with one call.
- **The first versions of three harness checks were wrong, not the library** (each worked out by hand before changing anything): the prime depth was set equal to the serve window and a commit was added before the first read (a correct overrun); a test ring was smaller than its window needed; and my expectation that BOTH readers count a phase mismatch in the first cycle was wrong (the first block of a cycle sees the other's old phase). A Swift helper trapped because `UnsafeMutableAudioBufferListPointer`'s subscript is bounds-checked against `mNumberBuffers`.
- **A TSan false positive in my first stress harness, found on the REAL code:** a write-after-read (the producer overwriting a slot the reader had read a lap earlier). The harness paced the threads with relaxed counters and standalone fences, which is valid C11 but invisible to TSan (it does not model standalone fences). Fixed in the harness by making reader-to-producer progress a real release/acquire edge, and keeping producer-to-reader progress deliberately fence-only so the library's own `w` stays the only edge TSan can see. The library was not changed.
- **ThreadSanitizer is blind to the ordering of relaxed atomics** (the production sample type): it passes a broken `w` protocol there (shown on M01 and M02: exit 0, zero warnings). That is why the TSan run uses the same file compiled with plain float samples and a stress in which a reader is never overtaken; there TSan reports the three ordering mutants as real data races (read in `SARingRender` against the producer's previous write) while the value checks of the same run say `0 failures` (the data happened to be right on this chip). Both facts are in `scripts/tsan-systemaudioring.sh`'s header.
- **Test gaps the first mutation run found in MY harness (all closed, then killed):** M14 (the unsigned-difference underrun test) survived because my underrun scenario committed before every render, so a reader was never further ahead than the producer at the moment of a call; M25 (prime without the `commits < T` guard) survived because the other guard (`depth > w`) hides it whenever commit sizes are equal; M07 (modulo `N-1`, consistently on both sides) survived because the only effect is on an UNPUBLISHED in-flight commit of exactly `maxCommit` frames at the edge of the window; M15 (post-copy check removed) survived the threaded stress even though about 1,500 blocks per run are torn, because a torn block only corrupts data when an in-flight commit lands on exactly the copied frames. Closed with scenarios K, F2, L and J, the last two via test hooks (`SA_RING_TEST_HOOKS`, compiled in only by the deterministic harness; the shipped object is unchanged).
- **Offline engine observation (OFFLINE ONLY, one run, printed by the test):** an `AVAudioEngine` in manual offline rendering with one source node pulled the block 12 times for 128 frames each (1,536 frames), every call had a valid sample time, none was silent, and the reader of the other channel (no node) was never called; the output was the ring's data for that channel (up to a mixer gain the test does not assume). **This says nothing about a real-time device, Bluetooth, or the order in which a real-time engine runs two blocks.**

### Verification record
- `swift build` (debug and release, no diagnostics in the new files, checked with a forced rebuild); `swift test` **593 tests** (470 Core + 83 Engine + 40 Tracking; +15 new in `SystemAudioRingTests`, none changed elsewhere); the same under `swift test --sanitize=thread`, no race reports (an earlier version of this note said that `--sanitize=thread` does not instrument C targets; that was wrong, corrected 2026-11-08, see "Step 6b-2 real runs", Open items).
- **Deterministic C harness** (`scripts/check-systemaudioring.sh check`): 4,376 exact checks (data per channel and frame index, both block orders, wrap, underrun, starvation and recovery at the cursor, overrun and its exact boundary, oversize, unusable output lists, the producer's refusals and flags, NaN/infinity/denormal/-0 bit for bit, prime, snapshot, phase and weaker rule, creation guards, torn and in-flight cases), 0 failures; also under UBSan.
- **Threaded stress** (producer thread, two reader threads, control-thread snapshots; no sleeps or clocks): paced mode, 3,000,000 frames, every block served and exact (23,437 per reader in the run); lapping mode, 2,000,000 frames with a 256-frame ring, readers overrun about 10,000 and torn about 1,500 times each in one run, no served block ever wrong; a 60,000,000-frame lapping run three times on the real code: 0 failures.
- **ThreadSanitizer** (`scripts/tsan-systemaudioring.sh`): clean on the real code (plain-sample paced, production-sample paced, production-sample lapping); **fails on the three ordering mutants** (race reports shown, exit 134), and the production-sample build is shown blind to them. **UBSan** (`scripts/ubsan-systemaudioring.sh`, recovery off, guard pages after every buffer, 32-bit-wrapping frame counts and byte sizes, absurd channel counts): clean on the real code, 90 hostile checks; it kills M31 (a 32-bit size product) alone.
- **Scans:** the block and the producer pass (record above).

### Mutation check (test-the-test)
The list was written BEFORE any ring code, harness or mutant existed (M01 to M37, file time 22:27:30 in the scratch directory); each patch must match exactly once. Changes since: **M17** was re-defined (the zero fill is one shared loop for all silent reasons, so "overrun not zero-filled" is M08; M17 is now "pre-copy overrun test removed") **when the runner was written, before any mutant ran**: the runner file was created at 22:50:30, the first mutant file of any kind (the dry run, which ran only M02, M08 and M15) at 22:50:39, and the first M17 variant file ever generated (first full run, 22:53:19) already carries the redefined mutation; no variant with the old definition exists. The additions, marked `*`, were added in the same writing, before any mutant ran: M11b (the withdrawn draft: a shared cursor advanced only by the block assumed to run second) and M38 to M47. (Evidence limit: the runner was first committed in `a34712d`, so what it contained at 22:50:30 is not preserved; the timestamps and the first variant files are consistent with the account above and are all there is.) **M42** was a sketched mutant, "`servedFrames` incremented by `frameCount + 1`", that I dropped while listing the additions, as one more counter mutant like M36 (my reason as far as I can reconstruct it; I did not run it to check). It was never written into the runner and never run: no M42 appears in any run log, result file or the committed runner. It is an UNTESTED mutant, neither killed nor shown equivalent, and the table's 47 entries are the ones that exist. Detectors: D1 deterministic harness, D2p / D2l threaded stress (paced / lapping), D3 TSan (plain-sample build; only M01 to M03 are TSan race REPORTS, every other D3 kill is a value-check failure inside the TSan build), D4 UBSan on hostile inputs, D5 `swift test`, D6 the zero-call scan (it killed nothing: no mutant adds a call). Every mutant was run against every detector.

**47 mutants: 45 killed, 2 survived, 0 did not compile. No mutant is labelled equivalent: the object code differed from the real one for every mutant (compared, not assumed), so no equivalence proof was available or needed.**
- **SURVIVORS: M21 (the producer's release fence removed) and M22 (the render's acquire fence removed).** Not equivalent: both change the object code (a `dmb` is gone), and M22 visibly changes behaviour (torn blocks fell about fourfold, from about 68,000 to about 17,000 per reader in 60 million frames, i.e. the second look at `w` is satisfied earlier). But no detector fails on either: not the deterministic harness, not three 60-million-frame lapping runs of each, not TSan (it does not model standalone fences), not UBSan. The reordering these fences prevent is a weak-memory effect that this chip did not show in 180 million frames per variant. **So the fences rest on the C11 argument in docs/ARCHITECTURE.md alone, not on any test.** A killer would be a litmus-style test on weak-memory hardware or a model checker; neither was built.
- Killed by exactly one detector class (so the others would not have caught it): M01, M02, M03 (TSan plain-sample only); M07, M15 and M30 (deterministic harness only, M07 and M15 through the hooks); M27 (the threaded runs and TSan, because only concurrency makes `w` move between two loads); M31 (UBSan only, through the guard page).

### Known limitations of Step 6b-1 (stated, not measured)
- Everything about a real tap, a real-time engine, Bluetooth and the real order, frame counts and timestamps of two blocks in one cycle is UNVERIFIED. Whether the sample time is valid in the real graph is unknown (it was valid offline).
- **Starvation after an underrun, and recovery is NOT implemented.** At equal producer and reader rate a reader that has gone ahead of the producer stays starved. That is not only a consequence of departure (i): the design's own underrun row (zeros, the cursor still advances by `F`, late frames dropped) implies the same behaviour unless the control thread re-primes. The control-thread re-prime and the render-side jumps (the overrun jump to `w - T`, the divergence jump) are PLANNED, not implemented here or anywhere; there is no recovery of any kind, so after the first underrun a run would stay silent. (CLAUDE.md now forbids any listening build or audible use of the ring until that recovery is implemented and mutation-checked.)
- **M21 and M22 (the two fences) are UNTESTED.** No detector fails when either is removed, so they rest on the C11 argument in docs/ARCHITECTURE.md alone. The margin argument is checked by the hooks and the stress; the fences by nothing.
- **The TSan evidence for M01 to M03 comes from the PLAIN-SAMPLE build (`-DSA_RING_PLAIN_SAMPLES`), not from the shipped (atomic-sample) build.** On the shipped build TSan passes those three broken variants (shown: exit 0, zero warnings). So TSan being clean on the real code says the publication order is right in the plain-sample compilation of the same source and that the shipped build has no OTHER race TSan can see; it is not TSan evidence about the ordering in the shipped build.
- Capacity 32768 and prime depth 4 are GUESSES; the real constants leave a factor of eight between prime depth and serve window, the test rings do not.
- Sample values are copied unchanged (a NaN or infinity in the tap reaches the output); stereo Float32 interleaved only; no handling of a format or rate change; lifetime and teardown (when the ring may be freed while the engine or the IO proc could still call it) are Step 6b-2's problem.
- The scan reads the release objects of one build configuration with one toolchain's output format, checks calls and not behaviour, and is a narrow exception to CLAUDE.md's rule.

**Not run.** Anything that touches a real tap, a real-time engine, a device or a camera; the block under `SACreateSourceNode` in a real-time engine; the wiring (6b-2); a muted tap; sleep / wake; a device or rate change; a CPU, latency or drift measurement.

## Step 6b-2 findings and verification record (2026-11-08): the ring probe
**Update (later on 2026-11-08): the real runs happened; see "Step 6b-2 real runs" below. The text of THIS section describes the state at the first commit, when nothing had been run on hardware.**
**Scope.** `--ring-probe` / `--ring-probe-simulate`: the real tap into the ring and a real-time engine at output gain 0, measurement only (docs/ARCHITECTURE.md "Step 6b-2"; docs/MANUAL_TEST_STEP6B2.md for Sanjai). Local commits only; nothing pushed. **IMPLEMENTED-UNVERIFIED.** What ran here: unit tests, offline manual-rendering engines, a SIMULATED producer and consumer on our own threads, sanitizers, scans, a mutation check. **What did NOT run: a real tap, a real-time engine, any output device, the permission prompt, a sleep, a device or rate change.** No latency, CPU, drift or sound figure exists. `Sources/CSystemAudioRing`, `scripts/scan-rt-block.sh`, `scan_rt_block.py`, `scan_rt_callback.py` and `rt-block-allowlist.txt` are unchanged (checked with `git status` before the commit); `--tap-probe` is untouched (a `--tap-probe-simulate 2` run still exits 0 and writes its report).

**Header facts checked (macOS 27.2 SDK) and what they do not say.** `AVAudioSourceNode.h`: `initWithFormat:renderBlock:` "can be called on realtime or non-realtime threads ... it is the client's responsibility to handle it in a thread-safe manner"; for a real-time engine "initWithFormat:realtimeSafeRenderBlock: is preferred" (macOS 27+, Swift-unavailable: not used). `AVAudioMixerNode.h`: `outputVolume` is "the mixer's output volume (0.0-1.0, inclusive)"; **nothing** about whether a source node is still pulled at 0. `AVAudioEngine.h`: `stop` "stops the audio hardware and the engine ... Stopping the engine releases the resources allocated by prepare"; `detachNode:` "If necessary, the engine will safely disconnect the node before detaching it"; the configuration-change text (the engine "stops itself", and "must not be deallocated from within the client's notification handler"). `AudioHardware.h` / `AudioHardwareTapping.h`: `AudioDeviceStop` "Stops IO for the given AudioDeviceIOProcID"; `AudioDeviceDestroyIOProcID` "Destroys an AudioDeviceIOProcID"; `AudioHardwareDestroyAggregateDevice` "asynchronous"; `AudioHardwareDestroyProcessTap` "destroys the given Tap". **None of them says** that a stop or destroy waits for a callback or render block that is already running, or that none can start afterwards; hence the real backend never frees the ring. `SACreateSourceNode` IS callable from Swift here (the 6b-1 tests and the probe call it and render through it offline). **Whether a real-time engine pulls source nodes at mixer gain 0 is not stated and not measured**; offline it does (10 of 10 renders, both readers).

**Facts found on the way (offline / simulated; none is a hardware result).** (1) `AVAudioSourceNode.pan` did not separate channels: each mixer output carried the sum of both mono nodes, so my first version's claim "left reader = left speaker" was false and was removed; the wiring is tested by muting one node at a time. (2) A standalone `AVAudioMixerNode` reads `outputVolume` 0.0; an engine's own main mixer reads 1.0, in manual and in device mode (not started). (3) In my first threaded engine test the consumer outran the producer thread in a debug build (8 served blocks, 40 underruns): a test race, not a ring bug; fixed with a bounded buffer. (4) The first run of the new scan-coverage guard caught a string in my own report text that named the API (string literals count as code); I reworded the string instead of loosening the rule. (5) `scripts/scan-render-api-coverage.sh` needed one departure from the brief: the header may hold the factory's declaration line (see ARCHITECTURE). (6) In this run's ASan and TSan builds the `CSystemAudioRing` object references the sanitizer runtime (10 ASan symbols, 14 TSan symbols), i.e. it IS instrumented in this build. **Correction (see "Open items" below): the older statement that `swift test --sanitize=thread` does not instrument C targets was wrong; the pose-mailbox finding in this file says the opposite, and all three C targets were since checked.** The 6b-1 caveat stands: TSan cannot see the ordering of the ring's relaxed atomics.

**The underrun reasoning, checked as asked.** My untested reasoning: "the slope stays meaningful after an underrun because each reader advances by frameCount on every call". **It held, in two simulated runs.** (a) Deterministic, virtual time, real ring and real source nodes (`anInjectedUnderrun...` test): producer +10,000 ppm and a 1 s stall at 10 s; the fill slope before the stall and after the underrun both within the test's tolerances of the injected 0.01 x 94 x 512 = +481 frames/s (10 % before, 3 % after), the fill negative at every sample from 12 s to the end of the 90 s run, no block served after the underrun began. (b) Real-time, our own threads (`--ring-probe-simulate 40 --ring-sim-ppm=10000 --ring-sim-underrun=8,1`, one run, recomputed from its JSON): slope **475.4 frames/s before** the stall (8 samples) and **484.9 after** the underrun (30 samples), expected about 480; fill negative at every sample from 10.5 s, final fill -27,648; 2,993 underruns, 758 served, 3,751 calls, both readers identical. **This is a property of the ring's arithmetic on a SIMULATED producer; it says nothing about a real tap.** One consequence worth knowing: a reader that is lapped (fill above the serve window) at the start stays lapped for the whole run too, because no cursor ever jumps forward.

- **TSan counts only because it fails on a broken variant:** with the ticker's `post` stripped of its lock, `swift test --sanitize=thread --filter DispatchRingTickerTests` exits 1 with four data-race reports (`wait()` and `post(...)`); unmutated it passes.
- **ASan and the early free (R07):** the simulated mode joins the render thread and the producer, stops the engine, and only then frees the ring; the test asserts the event order. With the free moved to the top of `stopEngine()` and the real-time test run 10 times under ASan: **it failed 10 of 10 runs; ASan itself reported a heap-use-after-free in 3 of them, the event-order assertion in the other 7.** So ASan alone is a lottery here (the threads wake every ~10.7 ms and the window is short); the order assertion is the dependable detector.

### Mutation check (test-the-test)
The list was written BEFORE any ring-probe code, test or mutant existed (R01 to R22, file time 11:42:17 in the scratch directory); **three entries were added after the code and tests existed but before any mutant was run** (file time 12:01:14): R08b (the runner-level fixed-sleep cadence), R23 (post-start gain read-back) and R25 (the first-pull wait needs both readers). The first mutant ran at 12:02:14. (Evidence limit: the two list files are scratch files, not committed; the final list is the table in `scripts/mutate_ringprobe.py` and below; the times are file times.) **R25's killer test (`theRunWaitsUntilBOTHReadersHaveBeenCalled...`) was written before the first run because I predicted R25 would survive my first fake (it called both readers at once); it is therefore not a gap the run found.** Detector: the ring-probe suites (`swift test --filter 'RingProbe|DispatchRingTicker'`); each patch must match exactly once, and the files are restored and compared byte for byte after every mutant (checked against a backup after both runs).

**Two full runs, and what the first one showed.**
1. *First full run (24 killed, 1 survived).* **R10 survived** (below). That run also showed a weakness of MY tests: under R02 the test process aborted on a force-unwrap (`Unexpectedly found nil`, `RingProbeEngineTests.swift:296`, `summary!` of a run that never started), so the tests after it never reported and the dedicated wiring test `eachSourceNodeCarriesItsOwnChannel...` did not appear in R02's list. R02 was still killed (a crash is a failure), and run alone the wiring test does kill R02 (shown), but a crash is a blunt detector that hides other verdicts.
2. *Fixes, then a second full run on the FINAL tests (25 of 25 killed).* R10: `RingProbeEngine.startAttempts` and an assertion on it. Crashes: every force-unwrap in the new tests became `try #require` or a NaN / zero sentinel, so a nil is a reported failure. The table is from this run.
